# CYBERPUNK 3D PORTFOLIO — MASTER BUILD SCRIPT

ROLE OF THIS DOCUMENT
This document is an implementation specification for an AI coding agent.
Do not treat it as a visual moodboard only.
Every scene must be implemented as an actual interactive 3D experience.
The portfolio must be driven by the engineer profile and projects supplied in the resume.
Do not invent employment history, projects, metrics, clients, awards or technologies.
Use illustrative technical data only when a visual animation requires sample payloads.

# CORE CREATIVE DIRECTION
The theme is CYBERPUNK, NOT SPACE.
Do not create planets, galaxies, nebulae, star fields, astronauts or outer-space environments.
The visual language should feel like a futuristic city, underground systems lab, neon data center and advanced interface combined.
Use wet black surfaces, smoked glass, translucent polymer, brushed dark metal, holographic panels, cables, rails, terminals and architectural grids.
The environment should feel physical and urban rather than cosmic.
Use neon cyan, acid green, electric violet and controlled white as accent colors.
Never use rainbow gradients.
Never turn every object into glowing neon.
Most surfaces should remain dark and restrained.
Glow is reserved for active data, interaction states and important edges.

# TECHNICAL REQUIREMENT
Use Next.js App Router.
Use TypeScript.
Use React Three Fiber for WebGL.
Use Drei for helpers and loaders.
Use GSAP with ScrollTrigger for scene choreography.
Use Framer Motion for DOM typography and interface overlays.
Use GLSL shaders for particles, distortion, displacement and transition effects.
Use react-three/postprocessing for bloom, depth of field and other post effects.
Use Lenis for smooth scrolling if it integrates cleanly with ScrollTrigger.
Use instanced rendering for repeated particles and repeated geometry.
Use dynamic imports for heavy 3D scenes.
Do not enable OrbitControls in production.
The camera must be controlled by the experience.

# IMPORTANT SCROLL RULE
Do not build a normal page where the WebGL scene simply sits behind HTML sections.
Scrolling must drive the 3D narrative.
The page can still use native scroll position as the timeline driver, but the visual result must feel like a sequence of cinematic scenes.
Each scene owns a normalized progress range.
GSAP ScrollTrigger controls scene progress.
Camera, object transforms, particles, postprocessing and DOM typography must all respond to the same scene progress.
Use power3.inOut, power2.inOut, expo.out and custom cubic-bezier easing.
Avoid linear easing for visible object motion.

# CAMERA RULES
Use one main PerspectiveCamera for the cinematic experience.
Use secondary cameras only when technically necessary.
Camera movement must be smooth and damped.
Do not snap the camera between scenes.
Use small camera shake only during major scene transitions.
Never use camera shake continuously.
Camera shake should be deterministic and subtle.

# CURSOR RULES
Create a custom DOM cursor.
The default browser cursor should be hidden on desktop.
The cursor has an inner dot and an outer ring.
The outer ring expands over interactive objects.
The ring changes shape slightly when hovering a 3D interactive object.
The cursor should not become so large that it blocks content.
Use raycasting for 3D hover detection.
Use throttled pointer calculations where necessary.

# PARTICLE RULES
Particles are not decorative stars.
They represent data, electricity, dust, signal fragments and system activity.
Use shader-based particles.
Use instanced or GPU-friendly rendering.
Particles should have depth fade.
Particles should have soft glow.
Particles should respond to cursor proximity.
Particles should react to scene progress.
At high scene intensity, particles should accelerate and spread.
At calm moments, particles should regroup into structures.

# MATERIAL RULES
Primary surfaces: dark smoked glass.
Secondary surfaces: black anodized metal.
Interactive edges: controlled neon emissive material.
Glass objects should use MeshPhysicalMaterial where appropriate.
Use transmission carefully; do not make every surface fully transparent.
Use roughness variation to create realistic glass.
Use subtle iridescence on hero objects only.
Chromatic aberration should be restrained.

# LIGHTING RULES
Use a dark environment.
Use a strong key light only when needed.
Use rim lights to separate black objects from the background.
Use emissive practical lights inside machines.
Use local lights near active data paths.
Do not wash the entire scene in blue.
Allow large regions of near-black negative space.

# TYPOGRAPHY RULES
Typography must be large and confident.
Use a modern grotesk or geometric sans-serif.
Headlines should feel architectural.
Use small uppercase technical labels for system metadata.
Avoid paragraphs floating inside 3D space.
Keep detailed text in readable DOM overlays.
3D text should be used sparingly.

# TRANSITION RULES
Every scene must have an identifiable transition mechanism.
Possible mechanisms include shutter, data tunnel, folding panel, disintegration, scanline wipe, mechanical rotation, glass fracture and liquid displacement.
Do not use the same transition for every scene.
Transitions should be driven by scene progress.
Transitions must preserve visual continuity.

# RESPONSIVE RULES
Desktop receives the full cinematic experience.
Tablet reduces particle count and depth complexity.
Mobile keeps the storytelling but simplifies expensive effects.
On mobile, cursor interactions become touch interactions.
Do not render thousands of expensive individual objects on low-power devices.
Preserve the scene hierarchy even when reducing detail.

# ACCESSIBILITY
Provide readable HTML equivalents for important text.
Do not place essential information only inside WebGL.
Interactive project links must remain keyboard accessible.
Respect prefers-reduced-motion.
When reduced motion is enabled, keep scene changes but remove aggressive camera and particle motion.

# PERFORMANCE
Use React.memo for static 3D components where useful.
Avoid recreating geometries and materials every render.
Use useMemo for expensive geometry.
Use useRef for animation targets.
Use GSAP contexts and clean them up.
Kill ScrollTriggers on component unmount.
Dispose geometries, materials and textures when appropriate.
Lazy load heavy project media.
Use compressed textures.
Prefer video textures only where they materially improve a project showcase.
Use requestAnimationFrame only where it cannot be replaced by the render loop.

# RESUME CONTENT SOURCE
Professional profile: backend-focused Software Engineer with 4 years of experience building and scaling Node.js services for high-volume e-commerce and enterprise platforms.
Core specialization: Shopify and ERP integrations, event-driven order processing, AWS SQS FIFO queues, REST and GraphQL APIs, production debugging, order/return/fulfillment workflows.
Primary current experience: Software Engineer at Nicobar Design Pvt. Ltd., March 2025 to Present.
Previous experience: Software Developer at Plexify Global Ind Pvt Ltd, October 2024 to February 2025.
Previous experience: Node.js Developer at Manthan IT Solutions, June 2023 to October 2024.
Certification period: Full Stack Developer certification, December 2022 to June 2023.


# SCENE 01 — THE NEON CORE — HERO / IDENTITY

SCENE PURPOSE: Introduce Ajit as a backend-focused software engineer through one central interactive cyberpunk machine.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- AJIT KUMAR
- SOFTWARE ENGINEER
- BACKEND • MICROSERVICES • E-COMMERCE • EVENT-DRIVEN SYSTEMS
- 4+ YEARS EXPERIENCE

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: A large suspended cybernetic core made from a transparent glass-metal torus, six rotating mechanical rings, inner luminous circuitry, and a small floating identity glyph. This is the main hero object.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: The object must react continuously to the pointer. Pointer proximity bends nearby particles, rotates the nearest ring toward the pointer, changes emissive intensity, and causes tiny mechanical components to unlock and relock.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: At the first scroll movement the core rotates 25 degrees on Y, then 18 degrees on X. With continued scrolling it moves toward the camera, grows from 1.0 to 1.45 scale, and passes partially through the camera plane before dissolving into the next scene.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Use layered cinematic motion with non-linear easing. The main object should never remain completely static.

SCENE TRANSITION: Use a digital shutter made from thin horizontal scanlines. The center core becomes overexposed for a fraction of a second, then the screen displaces sideways with a noise shader.

SCENE IMPLEMENTATION CHECKLIST:
1. Create the scene root as an isolated React Three Fiber component.
2. Create reusable geometry and materials outside the render loop where possible.
3. Register the scene with the global scroll timeline.
4. Give the scene a normalized progress value from 0 to 1.
5. Map camera position and look-at target to progress.
6. Map primary object rotation to progress.
7. Map primary object scale to progress.
8. Map particle intensity to progress.
9. Map DOM text opacity to progress.
10. Map DOM text vertical displacement to progress.
11. Map postprocessing intensity to progress.
12. Add a unique entrance animation.
13. Add a unique exit animation.
14. Add desktop cursor interaction.
15. Add touch-safe behavior.
16. Add reduced-motion behavior.
17. Ensure the scene can be unmounted without leaking GSAP timelines.
18. Ensure textures and geometries are disposed when appropriate.
19. Ensure text remains readable at normal viewing distance.
20. Test the scene independently before connecting it to the master timeline.

DO NOT:
- Do not turn this scene into a normal 2D section.
- Do not use generic floating spheres as the main visual.
- Do not fill the entire viewport with uncontrolled particles.
- Do not use random animation without a visual purpose.
- Do not invent resume claims.
- Do not use space imagery.
- Do not use excessive glitch effects.
- Do not make every object glow.


# SCENE 02 — THE ENGINEER PROFILE — HUMAN + SYSTEM

SCENE PURPOSE: Show the person behind the systems without turning the section into a conventional resume card.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- BACKEND-FOCUSED SOFTWARE ENGINEER
- Node.js • Express.js • Moleculer
- REST • GraphQL • Microservices
- Production debugging • scalable systems • integrations

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Create a giant vertical cyberpunk identification panel floating in front of a dark architectural grid. Around it, construct small 3D service modules representing API, database, queue, cache, webhook, and worker.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Hovering a module pulls it forward by 0.25 world units and draws a thin luminous connection from that module to the main identity panel. Moving away lets it return with spring easing.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: As the section enters, the camera travels from a three-quarter angle to a frontal view. The profile panel separates into layers: title, role, architecture, and capability. Each layer moves at a different depth.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Use layered cinematic motion with non-linear easing. The main object should never remain completely static.

SCENE TRANSITION: The profile panel folds into a vertical data blade. The blade rotates 90 degrees and becomes the first node of the architecture scene.

# SCENE 03 — MICROSERVICE CITY — ARCHITECTURE

SCENE PURPOSE: Turn backend architecture skills into a visible cyberpunk city of services.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- MICROSERVICES
- NODE.JS
- EXPRESS.JS
- MOLECULER
- REST APIs
- GRAPHQL
- ASYNCHRONOUS PROCESSING
- WEBHOOKS
- IDEMPOTENCY
- RETRY WORKFLOWS

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Build a futuristic miniature city where each tower represents a service. Towers are black glass with translucent interiors. Service names appear as tiny holographic labels, never as flat cards.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Pointer movement creates a local magnetic field. Nearby service towers tilt toward the pointer and brighten. Connections between related services become thicker when the pointer crosses them.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: Scroll moves the camera through the city rather than simply moving the DOM. The camera starts above the city, descends between service towers, then travels horizontally through the internal network.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Requests are represented by small luminous packets. Packets travel from API gateway to services, then to cache/database/queue nodes. No packet should move linearly; use curved bezier paths and power3.inOut easing.

SCENE TRANSITION: The camera follows one order packet. It accelerates through a tunnel and exits directly into the commerce system.

SCENE IMPLEMENTATION CHECKLIST:

# SCENE 04 — COMMERCE MACHINE — NICOBAR PLATFORM

SCENE PURPOSE: Make the strongest professional project the flagship technical story.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- NICOBAR E-COMMERCE PLATFORM
- CHECKOUT
- FULFILLMENT
- CANCELLATION
- RETURNS
- EXCHANGES
- GIFT CARDS
- SHOPIFY
- ERP
- REDIS
- MONGODB

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Create a huge cyberpunk order-processing machine. It contains a storefront gateway, order core, inventory chamber, fulfillment rail, return chamber, exchange chamber, gift-card vault, Shopify gateway, ERP gateway, Redis cache and MongoDB data vault.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Hover over a subsystem and isolate it. Dim every unrelated subsystem, enlarge the selected module, and show animated data packets entering and leaving it.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: Scrolling starts an order journey. The order packet enters checkout, moves to order processing, splits into inventory and payment-style internal routes, reaches fulfillment, and later branches into cancellation, return, exchange, and settlement paths.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Each stage must have a different physical metaphor. Checkout is a scanner gate. Fulfillment is a conveyor. Returns are reverse-moving packets. Exchange is a packet that transforms into a replacement packet. Gift cards are encrypted chips.

SCENE TRANSITION: At the end of the order lifecycle, the packet becomes a Shopify webhook event and launches into the next scene.

# SCENE 05 — EVENT RAIL — AWS SQS / RETRIES / DLQ

SCENE PURPOSE: Visually explain event-driven order processing, ordered delivery, deduplication, delay queues, retry/backoff and dead-letter handling.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- EVENT-DRIVEN PROCESSING
- AWS SQS
- FIFO QUEUES
- ORDERED DELIVERY
- DEDUPLICATION
- DELAY QUEUES
- RETRY
- BACKOFF
- DEAD-LETTER QUEUES

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Build multiple transparent cyberpunk queue rails. Each rail is a physical tunnel containing moving event capsules. Use different rail colors only for meaning: primary events, retries, and failures.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Hovering a message freezes it for inspection. Display a compact holographic diagnostic overlay with event type, sequence number, retry count and state. Do not invent business values; use illustrative labels.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: The first scroll sends an event through the FIFO rail. A duplicate event appears and is rejected by a deduplication gate. A failed event enters a delay chamber, waits, then retries. A repeatedly failing event eventually moves into the dead-letter chamber.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Backoff must be represented physically: every retry takes longer and the capsule travels through a larger loop before returning to the main rail.

SCENE TRANSITION: The final successful event exits the queue as a signed webhook packet and enters the Shopify integration scene.

SCENE IMPLEMENTATION CHECKLIST:
1. Create the scene root as an isolated React Three Fiber component.
2. Create reusable geometry and materials outside the render loop where possible.
3. Register the scene with the global scroll timeline.
4. Give the scene a normalized progress value from 0 to 1.

6. Map primary object rotation to progress.
7. Map primary object scale to progress.
8. Map particle intensity to progress.
9. Map DOM text opacity to progress.
10. Map DOM text vertical displacement to progress.
11. Map postprocessing intensity to progress.

# SCENE 06 — SHOPIFY NEXUS — API + WEBHOOKS

SCENE PURPOSE: Show Shopify Admin REST/GraphQL and webhook integration as a living synchronization system.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- SHOPIFY ADMIN API
- REST

- WEBHOOKS
- SIGNATURE VERIFICATION
- IDEMPOTENT HANDLERS
- RATE-LIMIT AWARE REQUESTS
- PRODUCT
- INVENTORY
- ORDER
- GIFT CARD

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Create a giant cyberpunk commerce gateway with a central hexagonal API core. REST requests use one visual path and GraphQL requests use another. Webhooks enter from the outside as signed data capsules.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Hover over REST or GraphQL changes the camera focus to that route. Hover over a webhook shows a signature-verification gate. Moving away returns the scene to its full-system composition.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: Scroll causes product, inventory, order and gift-card events to travel through the gateway. A webhook reaches signature verification, passes into an idempotency lock, then enters the event queue.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Show rate-limit awareness as a traffic meter. When the virtual request rate increases, the system automatically spaces requests instead of allowing chaotic flooding.

SCENE TRANSITION: The Shopify gateway rotates and becomes one side of a two-system bridge. The opposite side materializes as an ERP terminal.

# SCENE 07 — ERP BRIDGE — SOAP / CONTRACTS / SYNCHRONIZATION

SCENE PURPOSE: Visualize enterprise integration and SOAP-based ERP communication.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- ERP INTEGRATION
- SOAP APIs
- SOAP UI
- POSTMAN
- PAYLOAD DEBUGGING
- ORDER STATUS SYNCHRONIZATION

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Build two enormous cyberpunk terminals facing each other across a deep gap. One is the commerce side; the other is the ERP side. Between them is a flexible data bridge made from animated XML-like ribbons.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Pointer movement bends the bridge toward the cursor. Hovering the bridge reveals a transparent payload inspector. The payload should be abstract and safe: <Order>, <Status>, <Items>, <Reference>.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: Scroll moves the camera across the bridge. During the movement, one payload travels from commerce to ERP and a response payload returns in the opposite direction.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Payload debugging is represented by a scanning beam that travels through the data ribbon and briefly highlights malformed-looking segments before showing a corrected synchronized packet.

SCENE TRANSITION: The bridge collapses into a stream of data fragments that fall downward into a database landscape.

# SCENE 08 — DATA VAULT — MONGODB / MYSQL / ORACLE / REDIS

SCENE PURPOSE: Show database breadth without using ordinary database logos as decoration.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- MONGODB
- MYSQL
- ORACLEDB
- REDIS
- CACHING
- WORKFLOW STATE
- TRANSACTIONAL DATA
- QUERY PERFORMANCE

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Create four distinct cyberpunk data vaults. MongoDB is a flexible cluster of floating document shards. MySQL is an organized relational grid. OracleDB is a massive enterprise vault. Redis is a fast glowing memory layer positioned closest to the active request path.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Hovering a vault causes the camera to orbit that vault and reveals a stylized internal structure. Redis should respond fastest, with a short visual path from request to response.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: A request enters the scene. It first checks Redis. If the data is unavailable, it travels to the persistent database layer. The response returns and populates the cache.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Use thousands of tiny data particles inside the vaults, but keep the scene readable. Data should look like structured information, not stars.

SCENE TRANSITION: All four vaults compress into one data cube. The cube becomes the screen of a mobile device in the next scene.

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Create a long horizontal cyberpunk gallery containing large floating project slabs. Each slab is a rounded glass-metal rectangle with an embedded image/video area, project title, technology tags and a small project number.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Hover causes the card to tilt toward the cursor, move forward in Z, and reveal its technical stack. Add a subtle chromatic split around the edges. Never use an excessive glitch that makes the project unreadable.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: Vertical page scrolling drives horizontal camera movement. The visitor should feel as if they are walking through a physical project archive.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Cards must not all animate identically. The flagship commerce projects can have stronger motion; supporting projects can use quieter rotations and depth shifts.

SCENE TRANSITION: The gallery reaches its final project slab. The slab opens like a mechanical door and reveals a mobile device floating inside.

# SCENE 10 — MOBILE WEALTH NODE — PLEXIFY

SCENE PURPOSE: Show React Native, financial product interfaces, wealth management and the Hedera blockchain work from the resume.

EXACT CONTENT TO SHOW:
- PLEXIFY WEALTH APP
- REACT NATIVE
- INSURANCE
- BONDS
- PROPERTY
- USER ASSETS
- WEALTH MANAGEMENT
- WILL GENERATION
- REALM
- HEDERA BLOCKCHAIN

PRIMARY 3D OBJECT: Build a large futuristic mobile device floating vertically in front of the camera. The device has layered glass UI panels extending into 3D space. Behind it, create a blockchain lattice of connected nodes.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Move the cursor over different UI layers. The selected layer rises toward the viewer while the other layers move backward. Cursor movement should subtly rotate the device.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: The device rotates from portrait to a slight perspective angle. The interface layers separate, revealing assets, liabilities, property, insurance and will-generation concepts as visual modules.

ANIMATION / MOTION: The blockchain lattice should remain secondary. Nodes connect and pulse only when data moves between the app and the secure-data layer.

SCENE TRANSITION: The blockchain lattice expands until it fills the viewport, then its nodes rearrange into a role-based access hierarchy.

SCENE IMPLEMENTATION CHECKLIST:
1. Create the scene root as an isolated React Three Fiber component.
2. Create reusable geometry and materials outside the render loop where possible.
3. Register the scene with the global scroll timeline.
4. Give the scene a normalized progress value from 0 to 1.
5. Map camera position and look-at target to progress.
6. Map primary object rotation to progress.
7. Map primary object scale to progress.
8. Map particle intensity to progress.
9. Map DOM text opacity to progress.
10. Map DOM text vertical displacement to progress.
11. Map postprocessing intensity to progress.
12. Add a unique entrance animation.
13. Add a unique exit animation.
14. Add desktop cursor interaction.
15. Add touch-safe behavior.
16. Add reduced-motion behavior.
17. Ensure the scene can be unmounted without leaking GSAP timelines.
18. Ensure textures and geometries are disposed when appropriate.
19. Ensure text remains readable at normal viewing distance.
20. Test the scene independently before connecting it to the master timeline.

DO NOT:
- Do not turn this scene into a normal 2D section.
- Do not use generic floating spheres as the main visual.
- Do not fill the entire viewport with uncontrolled particles.
- Do not use random animation without a visual purpose.
- Do not invent resume claims.
- Do not use space imagery.
- Do not use excessive glitch effects.
- Do not make every object glow.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: Hovering a role level highlights only the permissions and routes belonging to that level. The selected level becomes physically closer while unauthorized routes fade into transparent wireframes.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: The camera climbs the hierarchy. Each level unlocks another layer of the tower. At the top, the entire system becomes visible as a structured enterprise network.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Use smooth data routing lines to show how a user request travels through authentication, role resolution, routing and database access.

SCENE TRANSITION: The hierarchy grid fractures into dozens of diagnostic windows, beginning the production debugging scene.

CURSOR / HOVER BEHAVIOR: The cursor acts as an investigation scanner. Moving over a panel brings it forward and increases readability. Clicking or hovering the failed packet opens a chain showing API request → service → queue → database → failure point.

SCROLL BEHAVIOR: Scroll advances the investigation step by step. First reveal the log, then the API payload, then the queue state, then the database record, then the root cause, then the corrected workflow.

ANIMATION / MOTION: Do not fabricate specific incidents from the resume. Use clearly labeled illustrative diagnostic data. The visual story should demonstrate the debugging method rather than claim a specific production outage.

SCENE TRANSITION: Once the issue is resolved, the red packet turns into a stable green/white signal and travels toward the final skills matrix.

# MASTER SCROLL TIMELINE

- Create one master GSAP timeline for the portfolio.
- Each scene receives a fixed scroll interval rather than competing for the same ScrollTrigger.
- Scene progress must be deterministic from scroll position.
- When scene progress is 0 the scene is in its entrance state.
- When scene progress is 0.5 the scene is fully readable and interactive.
- When scene progress is 1 the scene is prepared for transition.
- Do not let independent animation loops fight GSAP-controlled transforms.
- Idle animation may run only on properties that are not currently owned by the scroll timeline.
- During active scrolling, blend idle rotation into scroll rotation rather than abruptly replacing it.
- When scrolling stops, return gradually to idle motion.

# FRAMER MOTION DOM LAYER

- Use Framer Motion for large headings, labels, buttons and project metadata.
- DOM text must move independently in depth illusion using translateY, scale and opacity.
- Do not attempt to render all typography inside WebGL.
- Use AnimatePresence only for UI elements that genuinely enter and leave.
- Use viewport-safe typography sizes.
- Keep the primary heading visible long enough for the visitor to understand the section.
- Use staggered children only for grouped technical labels.
- Avoid exaggerated bounce animations.

# GLSL TRANSITIONS

- Create a reusable fullscreen transition shader.
- The shader must support progress from 0 to 1.
- Include procedural noise.
- Include directional displacement.
- Include scanline modulation.
- Include subtle RGB channel offset.
- Expose intensity as a uniform.
- Do not make the effect permanently active.
- Use the shader only during scene boundaries.
- Keep the final image readable before the transition reaches full intensity.

# CYBERPUNK ENVIRONMENT

- Use architectural geometry rather than outer-space geometry.
- Add distant buildings, conduits, cables, vents, panels and illuminated signage as background silhouettes.
- Keep background details low-poly or instanced to protect performance.
- Use fog sparingly to create depth.
- Use wet reflective surfaces where appropriate.
- Use subtle scanlines and grain over the final composition.
- Use small animated warning indicators as environmental details.
- Do not create a dystopian city that distracts from the portfolio content.
- The environment is supporting art direction, not the main subject.

# FINAL QUALITY BAR

- The website should feel like one continuous interactive film.
- A visitor should understand that the central subject is an engineer and his systems work.
- The technical content must remain legible and truthful.
- Every major animation must communicate hierarchy, architecture, workflow or interaction.
- Avoid template-like portfolio layouts.
- Avoid generic neon cyberpunk clichés when they do not support the engineering story.
- Prefer physical metaphors: rails, bridges, vaults, machines, terminals, access towers, control rooms and data cores.
- Every scene must have a clear beginning, middle and end.
- Every scene must visually hand off its main object or data signal to the next scene.
- The final build should prioritize polish and consistency over adding unnecessary effects.

# SCENE 01 — STATE MACHINE

STATE A — PRELOAD:
Load only the assets required for this scene and its immediate predecessor.
Keep the scene invisible while assets initialize.
Do not block the entire application on optional project media.

STATE B — ENTER:
Start from a deliberately composed state rather than an arbitrary hidden state.
Bring the camera into position with exponential damping.
Fade the scene environment in before the primary object reaches full brightness.
Reveal the main title after the main 3D silhouette is recognizable.

STATE C — INTERACTIVE:
Enable raycasting and cursor effects.
Enable hover reactions.
Keep interaction responses local to the object under the pointer.
Do not make unrelated objects move excessively when one object is hovered.

STATE D — SCROLLING:
GSAP becomes the primary owner of scene progression.
Blend idle motion into scroll motion.
Increase motion energy only where the storyboard specifies it.
Never make scrolling feel like a slideshow of unrelated animations.

STATE E — EXIT:
Disable expensive interaction effects when the scene leaves the active range.
Reduce particle count or intensity when the scene is far outside the viewport.
Finish the scene in a state that visually connects to the next scene.

STATE F — REDUCED MOTION:
Disable camera shake.
Reduce particle velocity.
Replace major object travel with controlled fades and small rotations.
Keep all text and project information available.

Do not make unrelated objects move excessively when one object is hovered.

STATE D — SCROLLING:
GSAP becomes the primary owner of scene progression.
Blend idle motion into scroll motion.
Increase motion energy only where the storyboard specifies it.
Never make scrolling feel like a slideshow of unrelated animations.

STATE E — EXIT:
Disable expensive interaction effects when the scene leaves the active range.
Reduce particle count or intensity when the scene is far outside the viewport.
Finish the scene in a state that visually connects to the next scene.

STATE F — REDUCED MOTION:
Disable camera shake.
Reduce particle velocity.
Replace major object travel with controlled fades and small rotations.
Keep all text and project information available.

# SCENE 13 — STATE MACHINE

STATE A — PRELOAD:

STATE B — ENTER:
